SPA: the invitations list (headed **Invitations**) asks for expired ones too and marks them **Expired** with a **Resend** button; every row's menu has **Resend invitation** (`members.manage`). A toast on success and the list reloads; without outgoing mail the new link shows in the same dialog as on creating an invitation (`InviteLinkDialog`, now shared); a 502 says the mail could not be sent and the invitation is unchanged. |
| 3.126 | Playbook dry run and outline | done | **Done 2026-11-22** (branch `feat/playbook-dry-run`, spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-22-playbook-dry-run-design.md`). `POST /orgs/{org}/playbooks/{id}/dry-run` (`template_id`, `host_ids` ≤ 500, `tags` ≤ 32 matching `^[A-Za-z0-9_.:-]{1,64}$`, `diff` default true, `draft` for editor playbooks): `jobs.run`, with a draft also `playbooks.write`; an ordinary manual template run with `jobs.check_mode` (migration 00115) and `jobs.dry_run` ({tags, diff, draft}, 00116), the options sealed in the payload; the service refuses a draft, tags or diff without check mode and check mode on anything but a manual run; no follow-ups, no chain, no retry (409), no run notifications. The claim never hands a check-mode job to a worker that does not announce `supports_check_mode` (older workers leave it queued) and fails impossible rows; the worker passes `--cmdline=--check --diff --tags='<tags>'` to ansible-runner and refuses check options on other kinds; `jobs.stats.host_counts` per host for every run. An older manager would run a dry run for real: stop every manager before upgrading (`docs/upgrades.md`). `POST /orgs/{org}/playbooks/outline`: plays, entries (section, kind, line, depth, tags, `runs_in_check_mode`, anything but a literal true counting as running for real), tags and counts, bounded (2 000 plays+entries, depth 64, 64 tags per list/entry, 512 in all, 256-byte texts). SPA: the playbook page's header (counts, tag chips, **Dry run…**), the Editor tab's **Outline** (click goes to the line), a **Dry run** tab (template, the template's hosts where they resolve in the browser, tag toggles, diff with a note on file contents, the unsaved draft, the tasks that run for real or a note why they could not be checked, the latest dry run of the whole run group with counts and per-task diffs, live), the runs lists' *Dry runs* filter and badge, the run page's banner, per-host counts and diffs in the event log. Real-Ansible check mode is proven by `internal/e2e` in CI (`e2e-ansible`) and `web/e2e/dryrun.spec.ts`. Loose ends: 3.129 |
| 3.127 | Playbook page: repository text and save conflicts | done | **Done 2026-11-23** (branch `feat/3.127-playbook-repo-text-and-conflicts`, spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-23-playbook-repo-text-and-save-conflicts-design.md`; owner's call: the text is stored at sync, not fetched on demand). (a) A sync job's claim sets `Job.want_playbook_texts`; the worker then ships each reported playbook file's text under the result stats key `playbook_texts` (path order, at most 512 KiB each and 4 MiB together, counted JSON-escaped) and the reasons for the rest under `playbook_texts_left_out` (`too_large`, `budget`, `not_utf8` incl. NUL bytes, `unreadable`). The manager strips both keys from `jobs.stats` for every sync outcome, re-checks every limit (the worker is not trusted), and stores the text on `repo_playbook_sources` (`content`, `content_left_out`, migration 00117); a Failed report keeps the previous texts, a playbook a cap left out keeps its earlier text. `GET /orgs/{org}/playbooks/{id}/content` (`playbooks.read`): path, commit ("" for a kept text), synced_at, content or `left_out` (`not_sent` for an older worker's sync or a path the last sync did not report); 404 before the first sync, 422 for an editor playbook. The Editor tab of a repository playbook shows it read-only with Check, Variables (plus the stored sources' count) and the Outline. (b) `playbooks.revision` (migration 00118) bumps on every text save; GET/PUT/create answer `ETag`, PUT takes an optional `If-Match` (stale or malformed → 412, as the templates; absent → saves). The page sends it; a 412 keeps the draft and shows a notice with *Show their changes* (diff), *Reload* and *Save mine anyway* (with the re-read revision, so a further save by them is refused again and announced). Version skew in `docs/upgrades.md`, including stopping older managers before a rolling upgrade. Deferred minors: 3.130 |
| 3.128 | Purge old unaccepted invitations | done | **Done 2026-11-23** (branch `feat/3.128-purge-old-invites`). `tenancy.InviteReaper` (the auth reaper's shape: hourly ticker, injected clock, advisory key 8318 so one manager sweeps) deletes, across organizations, every unaccepted invitation that expired more than `tenancy.ExpiredInviteListWindow` (30 days) ago — exactly the ones the list with `include_expired=true` no longer shows and that can no longer be resent. Query `DeleteStaleInvites`; accepted invitations stay as history. Its own reaper rather than a pass of `auth.Reaper`, because `tenancy` imports `auth`. |
| 3.129 | Loose ends the 3.126 reviews deferred | done | **Done 2026-11-23** (branch `chore/3.129-3.130-loose-ends`, plan `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-23-loose-ends-3.129-3.130.md`). Outline: unnamed entries carry `name: ""` and the SPA names them by kind in the UI language (blocks were `"block"` before); `async`/`poll` are keywords, `action`/`local_action` name the task after their module; tags are budgeted by bytes (1 MiB, half for inherited tags) on every path including scalar roles, cut outlines say `truncated`; the API has its own outline DTOs (`PlaybookOutline*DTO`). Dry run tab: offered hosts follow the template's `limit` (an undecidable or invalid limit keeps every host), chosen hosts are filtered against the offered ones, host names and group members are read through a pool of 4 (no batch route exists), outline parse errors and request failures read apart, a template load failure has a retry, the draft tick clears after a save, `prepared` diffs are cut at 20 000 lines, the queued hint does not tick on an unparsable time, live streams are capped at 4 zone jobs and the rest poll incrementally, zones without a report count as waiting. Run page: present-tense live banner, a host missing from `host_counts` shows a mark instead of labels, `EventRow` memoised. Server: the draft size is checked in the jobs service (one shared `varsource.MaxPlaybookTextBytes`), `internal/jobs/store_sql.go` split, `host_counts` adds up per host and the host lists name a host once. Was: Cosmetic or bounded, none unsafe. Outline: empty names for keyword-only tasks, `{role: ~}` and `import_playbook: [x]` (fall back to the kind); `async`/`poll`/`action` taken as an unnamed task's name; inherited play tags copied into every entry (up to ~32 MB of JSON from ~16 KB of tags) and `tagsOf` scanning a huge list item before its length check; counts stop at the cap without saying so; the outline's domain types are the published schema names. Dry run tab: the template's `limit` is not applied to the offered hosts (the server's 422 refuses); a sole template that changes under the form keeps the chosen hosts; one host-name request per explicit host and one member request per subgroup; any outline error reads as "does not parse"; templates failing to load leave the form unexplained; the draft box keeps its tick after a save; `prepared` diffs are only collapsed, not capped; the queued hint's timer spins on an unparsable `created_at`; one live log stream per zone job; the host count misses zones whose feed has not reported yet. Run page: the banner speaks in the past tense while the run is live; a host missing from `host_counts` shows labels among numbers; `EventRow` is not memoised. Server: the draft size is checked by the handler only (`ValidateYAML`); `store_sql.go` is over 400 lines; `host_counts` would overwrite if two inventory names mapped to one host |
| 3.130 | Loose ends the 3.127 reviews deferred | done | **Done 2026-11-23** (same branch as 3.129). Worker: `ctx.Err()` between files, a file that cannot fit the full budget is stat'ed and left out unopened, a containment test with a subpath, `EncodedTextLen` lost its dead error. Manager: a forged report naming a path as both read and left out no longer marks the fresh text kept; a playbook a limit left unread with no earlier text reads `unreadable`; the content route carries `from_earlier_sync`. `If-Match: *` saves on routes where the header is optional; routes that require it refuse `*` with a message saying a concrete `ETag` is needed (owner may revisit). SPA: a sync job's run page refetches the repo-derived queries when the job finishes; the content query retries network/5xx; an older server's missing route, the "not synced yet" 404 and the playbook's own 404 read apart (both 404 sentences pinned in Go); a failing re-read after a 412 shows the error. Tests: the stale-revision service case asserts text and revision. Version-skew notes in `docs/upgrades.md`. Was: Cosmetic or bounded, none unsafe. Worker: the text read has no `ctx.Err()` check after it and still opens files once the 4 MiB budget is full (a stat would do); the containment test covers subpath "" only; `EncodedTextLen` has an unreachable error branch. Manager: a forged report that both reads and leaves out a path blanks that text's commit; a playbook a cap left out with no earlier text reads as `not_sent`, whose sentence names other causes; "kept from an earlier sync" travels as `commit: ""` instead of an explicit flag (a record without a commit would read the same); `If-Match: *` answers 412 as malformed (as on every revisioned route). SPA: the repository text is not refetched when a sync job completes (only when one starts), like the other repo-derived queries; the content query does not retry transient errors; a new SPA against an older server reads the missing route as "not synced yet"; a throwing re-read after a 412 leaves an unhandled rejection. Tests: the service-level stale-revision case does not assert the text and revision stayed; a redundant `int64` conversion in `internal/playbooks/service_test.go` |
| 3.131 | Loose ends the 3.129/3.130 reviews deferred | done | **Done 2026-11-23** (branch `chore/3.131-loose-ends`, plan `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-23-loose-ends-3.131.md`). `GET /orgs/{org}/hosts` takes a repeatable `id` filter (org-scoped, at most 500, `limit` ignored in cursor mode when ids are given); the SPA reads host names 100 ids per request (URL length) with a per-chunk fallback to ids. Group members still use the pool of 4: no route lists several groups' members and the host list has no group filter. A tab-wide sync watcher (`web/src/api/hooks/syncWatch.tsx`) follows every sync started in the tab until it ends, then runs the shared `invalidateRepoDerived` (repos, playbooks, templates, jobs — also used by the start and the run page); it drops a job after three failed reads in a row, a 404 or 60 minutes (refreshing once), keeps it through a session lock and forgets it at a session reset; a reload or another tab is not covered. `useDeleteHost` invalidates host names; the dry-run host filter was already right for every listable scope (tests added); the repository-text 5xx test runs on fake timers. Version-skew note in `docs/upgrades.md`. Was: Cosmetic, none unsafe. A sync started from the repository list or a template's source panel is not seen finishing unless its run page is open, so the repository text and other repo-derived data stay stale until refocus or remount (no global job watcher exists). `useDeleteHost` does not invalidate the cached host names (up to 30 s stale). The dry run form filters chosen hosts only for a `listed` scope. The repository-text 5xx test waits through the real retry backoff (about 1.5 s) and could flake under heavy load. Host names and group members are fetched one request each (pool of 4) because the API has no batch read |
| 3.132 | Local status page on the agent | idea | Owner idea 2026-11-26: the agent can serve a small read-only web page (and a JSON variant) on the machine itself: agent version, the manager it talks to and whether it is connected (since when, last contact, last heartbeat), its identity (host name, organization, certificate valid until, next renewal), the checks it runs with their interval, last run and last result (values, errors), buffered results waiting for the manager, the local `agent.yaml` checks and secrets by name only (never values), and recent log lines. **Off by default**; switched on in `agent.yaml` (e.g. `status_page: {listen: "127.0.0.1:8742"}`) with bind address and port of the operator's choosing, optional basic auth or a token for a non-loopback bind, and a refusal to bind to a non-loopback address without one. No write actions. Needs a design round (what may be shown, Windows/macOS service user, firewall notes) |
| 3.133 | Proxmox VE check: node, storage and guest status | idea | Owner idea 2026-11-26. Not possible today beyond the generic checks (cpu, memory, disk, zfs, smart, service, script) on the node itself. A `proxmox` plugin on the node's agent that reads the local API (`pvesh get … --output-format json`, or the API on 127.0.0.1:8006 with a token held as an `agent.yaml` secret) and reports: node (pve version, cluster quorum and member count, CPU/memory/load as Proxmox sees them, subscription state, pending updates), storages (active, usage %, per storage), and guests — one item per VM/container (`qemu/101 web01`, `lxc/203 db`): state (running/stopped/paused), uptime, CPU and memory use against their limits, disk I/O and network, HA state, last backup age (vzdump) and whether the guest agent answers; optional `guests` filter and an `expected_running` list so a stopped guest that should run alerts. Open questions for the design round: agent on every node vs. one node for the cluster (duplicate reports), whether guests become their own Pulsix hosts (link a guest to the host its agent runs in) or stay items of the node, replication jobs, Ceph health, and permissions of the API token (PVEAuditor) |
| 3.134 | Assertions on snmp checks | done | From 3.87. The 3.87 grammar for `snmp`: subjects for the named `oids` values (`value("name")`, numeric or text by the value's type), `response_ms` and `uptime`; decide what an actual may show of a device's values, since v3 secrets and community strings must never appear. **Done 2026-11-26** (branch `feat/3.134-snmp-assertions`, plan `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-26-snmp-assertions-3.134.md`). The `assert.SNMP` dialect: `value("name")` takes every numeric and text operator (no `exists`), `==`/`!=` comparing numbers or text as the operand is a number or a JSON string (`Assertion.Numeric`); `response_ms` (the first request's round trip) and `uptime` (seconds) are numeric. The probe judges each entry after an up poll: a number compared as text by the exact decimal spelling the device sent (a Counter64 above 2^53 included; numeric comparisons are float64's, exact up to 2^53), an Opaque float widened through its float32 spelling (also in `snmp.value`: 0.15, not 0.15000000596046448), text compared as a number when it is a decimal number (UCD's `laLoad` strings) and otherwise the error "the device answered with text, not a number: compare it with a quoted string"; the text compared is what `snmp.text` shows (cleaned, at most 512 bytes); a missing object fails every operator as `absent`, any other read error is the assertion's own fixed error. Actuals show a number bare and text quoted, cleaned and cut to 128 bytes; the judge sees only the decoded values (never the secret), pinned by a test over v2c and v3 credentials and leaky library errors. Descriptor: `assertions` (format `snmpAssertion` with its SPA twin), `snmp.assertion`/`snmp.assertion_actual`, `oids` stays the `ItemParam` and the item hook asks for the assertion names too; refused at save: a `value("x")` no `oids` entry names, an assertion named like an `oids` entry. Migration 00135 and `rules.SeedRules()` seed `SNMP assertion failed` (warn) and `(critical)`. SPA: the editor offers the snmp subjects (a value compared with `==`/`!=` is written as a number when the field holds one; a number-like text is typed, and shown, in double quotes), the hint names the units (a TimeTicks value in hundredths, `uptime` in seconds, wrapping after about 497 days), the host page's assertion table covers snmp. Skew: snmp never runs on agents (it names a secret), so 3.87's unevaluated-assertion mechanism has nothing to catch; `judge.Unevaluated` covers snmp anyway. Left out: interface subjects (interface items are named by the device, unknown at save time; `snmp.if.status` already alerts per interface through rules) |
| 3.135 | Windows real-process test for script check values | planned | From 3.6j (security review finding 8). The batch and best-fit rules for values (`internal/scriptargs`: printable ASCII by default, cmd.exe metacharacters and delimiters refused for `.bat`/`.cmd`) are pinned by unit tests only. Add a test in the agent-windows CI job that runs a real `.bat` (echoing `%1`, `%2`, `%*`) and a small ANSI C program (reading `GetCommandLineA`/`argv`) with bound values, and asserts each value arrives as one argument, unchanged, and that refused values never reach the program. |

## v0.4 · Alerting

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 4.1 | Monitoring groups: tag selector (`role=db AND env=prod`) + manual members + exclusions | in progress | The selector half shipped in v0.3b-2 (`MonitoringGroupsPage`, `internal/checks/groups.go`'s `HostMatcher` — "which hosts does this selector match" — and `RuleDialog`'s own group-scoped rules), carried along with the rule engine rather than announced as its own row. Manual members and exclusions are genuinely outstanding: group membership today is a tag selector evaluated per request with no reverse index and no override table, which is also why v0.4a could not resolve a monitoring-group silence (4.13) |
| 4.1a | Nested monitoring groups: optional parent, membership mode within-parent or independent, priority; hosts stay many-to-many | planned | |
| 4.1b | Inheritance semantics: rules override (deepest wins, priority breaks ties, ambiguous ties flagged), check templates / routes / maintenance add up; effective-rules and route-union view per host | planned | This row's own ruling that routes add up, and the route-union view per host it names, shipped in v0.4b — see 4.1c. What is left here is specific to nested monitoring groups (4.1a, still `planned`): rules' own deepest-wins precedence and check templates adding up once groups can nest at all |
| 4.1c | Alert routes: which notification channels hear which alerts — severity and lifecycle-event selections, org / monitoring-group / host scope, routes add up rather than override (4.1b's own ruling) | done | v0.4b. `internal/alertroutes` (`Route`, `Validate`, `Service`, `MatchRoutes`/`Channels`, the SQL store) plus the follower (`Follower.Tick`, one coalesced message per channel per tick — decision 5 — on top of `internal/notify`'s outbox) and the suppression it reuses unchanged from `alerts.Mute` (maintenance, snooze — nothing reimplemented). API: six routes (`alert_route_handlers.go`), CRUD over `/orgs/{org}/alert-routes` plus the per-host union view `GET /hosts/{id}/alert-routes` 4.1b itself named. GUI: Settings → Alert routes (`AlertRoutesPage`/`RouteDialog`) and the host page's own `HostAlertRoutesCard`, naming every route that covers the host, showing what each one fires on, and saying so explicitly when none do. Decision 8's "reported ineffective" is there too: a route whose last channel was deleted survives (the link cascades, not the route) and both views say "Reaches nobody — no channel" rather than leaving an empty cell. That last part did **not** ship with the rest of v0.4b — the whole-branch review found the decision promised twice in the spec and built nowhere, and it landed in the review's own fix wave. `TestAlertRoutesEndToEnd` (`internal/e2e`) drives a host's cpu and memory tuples into crit and asserts one delivery row for the channel naming both alerts |
| 4.2 | Check templates attached to monitoring groups | idea | Probably redundant, not merely unstarted: v0.3b-2's rule engine already binds rules to a monitoring group through its own precedence resolution (org → group → host → item), which is what a "check template attached to a group" would exist to do. A second template mechanism on top would duplicate that rather than add to it. Left as `idea` rather than `planned` until someone identifies a concrete gap the rule engine's own precedence does not already cover; if none turns up, this row should be closed as redundant rather than ever marked `done` |
| 4.3 | Alert rules: thresholds, duration, severity, per host / group | done | Shipped in v0.3b-2, not v0.4 — this row was stale (`planned`) through two milestones after the fact. Evidence: `internal/rules` (`for_duration`, severity, org/group/host/item precedence, the expression and form-mode editors, the fleet/host pages). The seeded `service` check watching `sshd` **must not ship an alerting rule enabled by default**. The seed stands because every host Pulsix manages is reachable over SSH, so the unit exists on the overwhelming majority — and on a host where it does not, v0.3b-1 shows one visibly odd entry and nothing else, because this cut has no alerting at all. The moment a default rule exists, that same harmless entry becomes a page at 3 a.m. for a host that is working perfectly |
| 4.4 | Notification schedules: time windows, weekdays, on-call rotation | done | v0.4c (design doc `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-13-v0.4c-notification-schedules-design.md`, including its own "As built"). Two separate halves, both on the route: the **delivery window** — `window_cron`/`window_duration`/`window_timezone` on `alert_routes` plus a breakthrough severity set that crosses a shut one, evaluated per route in `resolution.windowOpen` (`internal/alertroutes/follow_resolve.go`) against `maintenance.ActiveAt`, held as the ledger's new `deferred` state and caught up once the window opens (or never, if another route already delivered the same event — the window gate is per route, the ledger one row per event, see the spec's "As built"); and the **on-call rotation** — the new `internal/oncall` package (`Rotation`, `Exception`, `ChannelAt`, `Shifts`), a cycle of channels plus stand-ins, resolved at delivery time rather than alert-open time (decision 8) and usable as a route's channel source instead of its fixed list (`rotation_id`, `channelsOf`). Migration `00054`. API: nine routes for rotations and their stand-ins/roster (`oncall_handlers.go`), plus the window and rotation fields riding the existing `alert-routes` routes. GUI: `RotationsPage`/`RotationDialog` with a roster strip, and `RouteDialog`'s own delivery-window and channel-source sections. `TestOncallEndToEnd` (`internal/e2e`) drives a real agent's alert through a shut window, advances the follower's own clock past both the window opening and a rotation handover, and asserts delivery to whoever is on call at that instant, not whoever was when the alert opened |
| 4.5 | Channels: email (SMTP) | done | Shipped in v0.2d (`notify.KindEmail`, `internal/mail`), two milestones before this row was corrected — v0.4a's own design doc caught the drift while surveying what "alerting" already contained |
| 4.6 | Channels: generic webhook with template | done | Shipped in v0.2d (`notify.KindWebhook`) — covers n8n, Discord, ...; same stale-row correction as 4.5 |
| 4.7 | Channels: Telegram, Slack, Mattermost native | done | **Done 2026-11-26** (branch `feat/4.7-webhook-presets-slack-mattermost-telegram`, spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-27-webhook-presets-design.md`, plan `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-26-webhook-presets-4.7.md`). Built as decided below: migration 00136 adds `notification_channels.preset` ('' raw, `slack`, `mattermost`, `telegram`; a second CHECK keeps it on webhook channels). `internal/notify` gained the `Preset` interface (`CheckURL`, `CheckSettings`, `Render`), `Compose(Event) Message` — the service-neutral title, level, defused and clipped lines and link every chat renderer starts from — and the helpers `ChatText`, `Clip`, `Fit`, `MoreNote`, `SafeLink`; one package per service under `internal/notify/presets/` with `presets.Registry()` wired by `internal/app` and `apitest` (a test pins the registry to the schema CHECK), and `internal/notify/notifytest` holds the shared fixtures and golden-file helper. Every renderer also fits a 40 KiB byte budget, so the widest digest or batch stays under the 64 KiB body cap. A preset channel sends POST, takes no body template and no Content-Type header (the dialog leaves such a row out while a preset is chosen); headers and signing stay; changing the preset needs the URL again. Slack: the title as message text, an attachment in the level colour with mrkdwn sections (≤ 10 × 3000; formatting markers in chosen text swapped for look-alikes), context and an "Open in Pulsix" button; Mattermost: the legacy attachment (colour, title linked to Pulsix, Markdown text ≤ 12000, footer), no button (needs an integration callback); Telegram: `sendMessage` in **HTML parse mode** rather than MarkdownV2 (four escapes instead of eighteen), a level mark, ≤ 4096 UTF-16 units, previews off, the URL path must be `/bot<id>:<token>/sendMessage` on any host (refusals never repeat the URL) and `chat_id` is a number or a public @username. Level: the highest alert severity in the alert list order, a closed alert counting as ok. API: `webhook.preset` and `webhook.chat_id`; GUI: a Format select in the webhook dialog. Left out: e-mail and the raw webhook stay in `internal/notify` (moving them is 4.8's split), no Slack host check (compatible receivers, relays), Telegram forum topics, threads, mentions, editing a message on resolve. Was: **Design approved 2026-10-19, not yet written as a spec — these decisions live only here.** Finding that shaped it: all three are HTTP POSTs, and the existing `webhook` kind already does method, sealed URL, sealed header values, a body template and HMAC signing — so a user can reach Slack today by hand-writing a template. What "native" buys is the absence of that handwriting, not a transport. **Decision 1: presets on the webhook kind, not three new kinds.** A `preset` column (slack / mattermost / telegram, empty = today's raw mode), chosen over first-class kinds because the latter would touch the CHECK, `View()`, `Send()`, the GUI kind picker and every channel picker in routes, rotations and escalation steps — and again for each of 4.8's four services. Precedent: inbound webhook triggers already carry `preset` (github, gitlab) on one kind. **Decision 2: a preset owns the body**, so the template field disappears when one is set; two sources for one body is the duplication that has bitten this repo twice. **Decision 3: rich per-service rendering**, chosen over reusing `renderEmail`'s plain text — Slack blocks with an attachment colour by severity and a button to `Event.URL`, Mattermost sharing Slack's attachment format but getting its own tests because "compatible" is a claim until proven, Telegram Markdown with `chat_id` as a non-secret config field and the bot token inside the sealed URL. **Decision 4:** where one event batches alerts of mixed severity, the highest decides the colour — the convention the alert list already rolls up by. Unchanged by all of it: delivery, outbox, retries, signing, and every channel picker, because the kind stays `webhook`. The scope is deliberately shaped so each of 4.8's services is one renderer plus one line of validation |
| 4.8 | Channels: ntfy, Pushover, Twilio SMS, PagerDuty | planned | Additive on the existing `notify.Kind` interface; one bounded task each, no design needed. Before adding them, split `internal/notify`'s channel code into one package per channel (email, webhook, then the new ones), the layout 3.52 gave the agent plugins (decided 2026-11-12) |
| 4.9 | Escalation chains (after N min unacknowledged → next channel) | done | Shipped in v0.4d, tasks 1-9. Each route carries an ordered ladder of steps beyond its own immediate delivery (`alert_route_steps`, `alert_route_step_channels`), each naming a delay and either a fixed channel list or an on-call rotation (`alertroutes.Step`, `ValidateSteps`). The follower's `Tick` gains a second, time-driven pass (`alert_escalations`, an anti-join ledger, the same shape v0.4b's delivery ledger uses) that fires the next unfired step once its delay has elapsed since the previous step's own `fired_at` — never from when the alert opened (design decision 3). Acknowledging, closing, the severity leaving the route's set, disabling the route, deleting the route, and every mute reason (snooze, silence, maintenance) each stop or pause the chain per decision 4's table, with resuming giving the next step its full delay again (decision 5, amended by decision 8 so a severity re-entry does not get a fresh delay after a fall that stays within the route's set). A new `escalated` `alert_events` kind makes each rung visible on the alert's own timeline, not only in the channel it reached. API: the route DTO's `steps` field, validated the same way the route's own four selections already are. GUI: the route editor's ladder (add/remove/reorder a rung, pick its channel source) and the host card's escalation display. End to end: `TestEscalationEndToEnd` (`internal/e2e/escalation_test.go`) drives a real agent's crit alert through a base delivery and an escalated rung, proves the rung fires exactly once and the base channel is not re-notified, then proves an acknowledgement stops the chain before its next rung's delay elapses. This sub-project also settled 4.36 — see that row. One known limitation recorded as 4.41: the escalation clock samples a maintenance occurrence at two instants rather than over an interval, which can fire a rung slightly early after a short maintenance window, never late and never skipped entirely |
| 4.10 | Acknowledge, snooze, silence | done | v0.4a. Acknowledge and unacknowledge (`alerts.Service.Acknowledge`/`Unacknowledge`, voided by a worsening severity — decision 2); snooze until an instant or until the state next changes (`Service.Snooze`/`Unsnooze`); silence as a maintenance window with `reason = 'silence'` (migration 00050, `Mute`'s total suppression order over snooze/silence/maintenance). GUI: the Alerts page's row actions and dialogs (`AcknowledgeDialog`, `SnoozeDialog`). Silencing a monitoring group specifically is not included — see 4.13 |
| 4.11 | Maintenance windows on host / group / org + central list, visibility internal or public on selected status pages | in progress | The core (windows on host / inventory group / org, a central list) shipped in v0.2d (`internal/maintenance`); v0.4a added silencing on top (`reason = 'silence'`, 4.10) and the `monitoring_group` target kind at the schema level (not yet reachable — see 4.13). Still outstanding: "visibility internal or public on selected status pages" waits on v0.5's status pages existing at all |
| 4.12 | Alert history and timeline per host | done | v0.4a. `GET /orgs/{org}/alerts/{id}` returns one alert's timeline — its own `alert_events` interleaved with the `check_state_changes` transitions in `[opened_at, closed_at]` — rendered by `AlertTimeline`/`HostAlertsCard`'s per-alert disclosure on the host page. Scoped to one alert's own episode, by design decision ("Explicitly not in this sub-project"): a host-level timeline spanning every alert a host has ever had is 4.15, not this row |
| 4.13 | Monitoring-group silence resolution | idea | v0.4a widened `maintenance_windows.target_kind` to accept `monitoring_group` (migration 00050) and `alerts.Mute` accepts the kind in its own Go types, but nothing resolves a monitoring group's *membership* to a set of hosts for suppression purposes — groups are a tag selector evaluated per request (`checks.HostMatcher`, "which hosts match this selector", with no reverse direction), not a materialised membership table. Resolving it per alert means one selector evaluation per candidate group per `Mute` call, with a caching question attached. The API's own huma enum (`maintenance_handlers.go`, `calendar_handlers.go`) deliberately stays `enum:"host,inventory_group,org"` until this lands, so no window of this kind can be created today — an accepted gap, not a silent lie: an operator who wants to silence a monitoring group silences its hosts or its inventory group instead |
| 4.14 | Bulk acknowledge | idea | Deliberately not built in v0.4a (design doc, "GUI": "Deliberately not built"). Decision 4 already took the peak off an alert storm — `unknown` opens one host-scope alert, never one per tuple — so a storm of individually-crit tuples is the remaining case this would help with. A backlog row, not a rebuild, if it turns out to be missing in practice |
| 4.15 | Host-level state history (an aggregate timeline across a host's own alerts) | idea | Deliberately not built in v0.4a (design doc, "Explicitly not in this sub-project": "A history table for host-level state, so a host alert's timeline consists of its own events only"). Today a host's own alert history is reachable (`HostAlertsCard`'s "Full history, including closed alerts" link, seeded via `?host=`), but each alert's timeline still only ever shows that one episode's own events — there is no single view answering "show me everything that happened to this host's monitoring state, across every alert, in order" |
| 4.16 | A lighter, acknowledge-only permission | idea | v0.4a design doc, "API and permissions": acknowledging and snoozing require the existing monitoring write permission (`checks.write`); v0.4a introduces no new one. An ack is arguably a lighter act than rewriting thresholds or editing a check, and a separate, narrower permission (an operator can say "I'm on it" without being trusted to reconfigure monitoring) would be defensible — explicitly left as a backlog question rather than grown into the permission model under this milestone |
| 4.17 | Pulsix section in the end-user documentation (`NoiXdev/noix-docs`) | done | **Done 2026-11-11.** The Pulsix section is published: branch `pulsix` was merged into the docs repo's `main` (NoiXdev/noix-docs 21d1593), with its own sidebar topic and the `/pulsix` redirect. No Pulsix section exists in the docs repo at all — `find src/content/docs -ipath "*pulsix*"` returns nothing, while every other NoiXdev product has one, across v0.1 through v0.4a. CLAUDE.md's standing instruction is to update the matching docs page in the same work item a user-visible feature ships in, but there has never been an install, getting-started or concepts page for a first feature page (alerting, this milestone) to sit beside — writing one now would be an orphan addressed to a reader with nowhere to have come from. This row is the section itself: whoever picks it up should plan install/getting-started/concepts first, with alerting (what an alert is, when it opens and closes, acknowledging, snoozing, silencing through a maintenance window, reading the timeline) as the first feature page once that foundation exists |

## v0.4a follow-ups

Deferred decisions and limits recorded while building the alert lifecycle
(2026-10-06). Each was found during implementation and is a conscious "not
in this milestone", not an oversight left in a commit message. Source:
`.superpowers/sdd/2026-10-06-v0.4a-alerts/progress.md`.

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 4.18 | No test exercises `alert_events.kind`'s own CHECK enum boundary | done | **Already covered when checked, 2026-10-26 — no code written.** `TestEventKindsMatchTheSchema` (`alert_test.go`) does more than the row asks: it READS the migrations and parses their CHECK lists rather than comparing against a hand-written copy, pins `EventKinds()` against `alert_events.kind` exactly and in order, pins `alert_route_events.kind` as EventKinds() minus `escalated` (with its own explanation of why a route must never be able to select that kind), and rejects an invented one through `ParseEventKind`. The row is also stale on the count: there are eight kinds, not seven — migration 00056 widened the CHECK for `escalated`, and that widening is precisely what an earlier version of this test slept through, which is why it now reads 00056 rather than 00049. The only thing left unexercised is an actual INSERT per kind against a live database, which would prove nothing the parsed CHECK list does not already state |
| 4.20 | `Reconcile`'s `closeOrFlap` takes a `found bool` only to return `nil` | done | 2026-10-26. The guard is at the call site now, which also makes the two branches of that switch symmetric: an ok tuple with no open alert is nothing to do, read in the same place as a warn tuple with no open alert being an open. `closeOrFlap`'s doc comment says it is only ever called for a tuple that HAS one |
| 4.21 | `alerts` store: two sources of truth for the default mute reason | done | 2026-10-26. `normalize` (validate.go) reads through `reasonOrDefault` (store_sql.go) now instead of restating `if r == "" `. One rule, one statement: with two, the copy that moved would have decided the default by which path a caller happened to take — the service's validated write, or a `Window` built directly for the store. `reasonOrDefault`'s own doc comment names it as where that decision lives for the package, so the next reader looks in one place |
| 4.22 | `normalize`'s acceptance of `Reason=silence` is never exercised directly | done | 2026-10-26. The two tests around it covered the default (an empty reason becomes maintenance) and the refusal (anything else is `ErrInvalidInput`); nothing drove the arm that matters most. Deleting `ReasonSilence` from the switch would have sent every silence window to the default branch and had it refused as an unknown reason — with the suite still green, because the refusal test passes for the wrong input and the default test names no reason at all. Verified negatively: removing the arm fails the new test |
| 4.23 | `maintenance.Store.Update` with `TargetMonitoringGroup` has no round-trip test | done | 2026-10-26, and the round trip the row asked for is the WEAKER half of what it needs. The three target columns are mutually exclusive, so retargeting has to SET all three — the two being left to NULL and the one being moved to — and a monitoring-group window created and updated in place never exercises that clearing at all. The test changes the KIND on every leg (host → monitoring group → organization) and reads the row's own three columns, because the DTO cannot show a leftover: `Get` resolves one `TargetID` out of whichever column matches the kind, so a row claiming two targets at once would read back correctly. It also pins that the silence reason survives a retarget, and that an unknown monitoring group is refused on Update exactly as on Create |
| 4.24 | `alerts` store: writes history for rows it may not have changed | done | 2026-10-26, as the row prescribed — `RaiseAlertSeverity`, `SetAlertFlapping` and `CloseAlert` are `:execrows` now and a zero-row result is `ErrNotFound`, so the no-write case is unreachable by construction. **The event write was not the guard it looked like:** `alert_events.alert_id` references `alerts(id)` ALONE, not `(org_id, id)`, so an alert belonging to ANOTHER organization left the UPDATE empty while the event insert SUCCEEDED — a history row filed under the caller's organization about an alert it never touched. `ActionFlapped` writes no event at all (there is no "flapped" kind), so its no-op was invisible even in principle. Only the severity action was already covered, by the `GetAlertSeverity` read in front of it — which answered a raw `no rows` rather than `ErrNotFound`, so a caller matching on the sentinel had to know which action it had asked for; it gives the same answer as the other two now. The existing cross-organization RLS test got sharper on the way: it can assert "this matched nothing" outright instead of inferring it from a column that did not move |
| 4.25 | `alerts` store: `GetAlertSeverity` re-reads a value the write could report itself | done | 2026-10-26. `RaiseAlertSeverity` is `:one` now and returns the severity it replaced, so `GetAlertSeverity` is gone — one statement instead of two per `ActionSeverity`. The window was real, not just a round trip: the two statements run inside the evaluator's transaction under READ COMMITTED, so a commit in between left the SELECT's answer stale while the UPDATE saw the newer row, and the `severity_raised` decision hung on the stale one. The row's own suggestion — plain `RETURNING` — could not express it, because `RETURNING` yields the NEW values; a `FROM alerts AS prev` self-join could not either, since its scan keeps the statement's snapshot while the UPDATE's EvalPlanQual re-reads the row, which is the same disagreement moved inside one statement. What ships is a `WITH prev AS (SELECT … FOR UPDATE)` CTE: the lock makes the re-read the row this statement then overwrites. PostgreSQL 18's own `RETURNING OLD.severity` says it directly and should replace the CTE once sqlc can parse it — 1.31.1 answers `column "severity" does not exist`. Zero rows is `pgx.ErrNoRows` now rather than a row count, mapped to the same `ErrNotFound` 4.24 gave the other three writes. `TestRaiseAlertSeverityReportsTheSeverityItReplaced` pins the contract; returning the new value instead was verified to fail it and `TestExecuteRaisesPeakButNeverLowersIt` both |
| 4.26 | `internal/alerts/store_sql.go` is past the file-size split signal | done | 2026-10-26, in THREE cuts rather than the one the row names — it was written at 653 lines and the file had reached 912 (partly from 4.24 and 4.31 landing in it), so `store_sql_read.go` alone would have left 670 behind, still over the signal. The dividing line turned out not to be read-versus-write but **whose transaction it is**: `store_sql.go` 365 (contract, cursor codec, conversions), `store_sql_execute.go` 218 (the evaluator's write path, all of it inside the CALLER's per-host claim), `store_sql_read.go` 240 (what the API and the notification follower read, on the pool), `store_sql_actions.go` 162 (the four operator actions, each opening a transaction of its own — which is precisely why they are not more of the execute file). `OpenForHost` is formally a read and stays with `Execute`: it is the input those actions are computed from, on the same transaction, and splitting the pair would hide that they are one exchange. **The test files are deliberately left:** the row mentions `store_sql_test.go` in passing (653 then, 930 now) and `store_sql_task6_test.go` is 1336, but the package already carries eight test files grouped by milestone, so an ordering principle exists; re-cutting them is churn with no reader it helps, and the row prescribes no seam for them. If that is wanted it deserves its own row and its own argument |
| 4.27 | `alerts.Detail` re-declares `Alert` and `Suppression` instead of embedding `View` | done | 2026-10-26. `Detail` embeds `View`; a Detail IS a View with a timeline. Field promotion means every reader is unchanged — `d.Alert`, `d.HostName` and `d.Suppression` read exactly as before — so the whole change is the struct and the one literal that builds it. What it buys is the failure the row names: a field added to `View` no longer misses `Detail` until someone remembers to add it twice |
| 4.28 | `occurrenceAt` re-implements `maintenance.ActiveAt`'s own interval test | done | 2026-10-26. The one-off branch builds a `maintenance.Window` and calls `ActiveAt`, the same way the recurring branch beside it already did; the hand-written `now.Before(start) \|\| !now.Before(end)` is gone. Whether a stretch is half-open at its end is a convention `internal/maintenance` owns, and stating it twice gave the two somewhere to drift — an operator would have seen a window cover its final instant on the alerts page and not on the calendar. It also inherits 4.29's treatment for free: a one-off row with no start or end is now reported as unevaluable rather than read as "not now", which is what `ActiveAt` already said about it |
| 4.29 | A maintenance or silence window whose cron or timezone stops parsing goes silently inactive | done | 2026-10-26, and **the row's first half was already out of date**: `HostsInMaintenance` does not fail closed silently — it returns `evaluate maintenance window %s: %w` and fails the run loudly. The real remaining case was the quiet one the row names second. `occurrenceAt` (`internal/alerts/store_sql.go`) answered "inactive" for both "this window does not cover now" and "this window can no longer be evaluated at all", and nothing recorded the difference — so a bad cron un-muted every alert the window covered and the only trace was the page itself, during a silence an operator had declared. It returns an error alongside the bool now, and `WindowsForHost` logs it per window and carries on: suppression stays best-effort, because failing the call would take alerting down for the whole host over one bad row, but it is no longer silent. The log names the window, the organization, the host and the parse failure, and says the CONSEQUENCE ("is suppressing nothing") rather than only that something failed — the test asserts that wording, since a line saying only "error" would leave the operator exactly where they started. A missing timezone or duration on a recurring row is reported the same way and names which half is absent. Pinned against a real database with three windows — a bad cron, an unknown zone and a healthy one — because the third is the claim that matters as much as the first two: one broken row must not take the others down |
| 4.30 | The suppression CTE and `maintenance.HostsInMaintenance` disagree on which hosts a group covers | done | Settled with the v0.4a final review's Important #2: the divergence is correct and now documented on both sides. The CTE (`ListMaintenanceWindowsForHost`) covers every member because an unmanaged host still reports checks and still raises alerts; `HostsInMaintenance` covers managed hosts only because a run that cannot touch a host has no reason to be guarded on its account. Each doc comment names the other |
| 4.31 | `alerts` store: a zero-row update means two different things | done | 2026-10-26, and the shape was worse than the row recorded. Only `AcknowledgeAlert` and `SnoozeAlert` carry `closed_at IS NULL`, so only those two are ambiguous — and **each guessed the other's meaning**: Acknowledge always answered `ErrInvalidInput` ("alert is closed"), Snooze always `ErrNotFound`, so each was wrong in exactly the case the other got right. It also reaches an operator, contrary to the row's "handlers map both to the same response": `alert_handlers.go` maps `ErrInvalidInput` → 422 and `ErrNotFound` → 404, so acknowledging an id that never existed answered 422 about an alert being closed, and snoozing one that had just closed answered 404 about an alert plainly on screen. Both now read the row on the zero-row path only — inside the same transaction as the failed update, so it costs nothing normally and cannot disagree with it. `Unacknowledge`/`Unsnooze` are deliberately untouched: no `closed_at` predicate, one meaning, `ErrNotFound` already right — and the test pins that they still SUCCEED on a closed alert, since a guard copied across by symmetry would take away the last chance to undo a mistaken acknowledgement on an episode that has ended |
| 4.32 | `AlertSink` orchestration tests: `stubTx` embeds a nil `pgx.Tx` | done | 2026-10-26. `stubTx` implements the interface — eleven one-line methods — instead of embedding a nil. "Never actually used" was a property of today's tests, not of the type: the first orchestration test to reach for the transaction would have failed as a nil-pointer dereference inside pgx, with a stack saying nothing about why. Each method panics by name now, and the message names the decision the author then faces — a real transaction through `testdb`, or a real behaviour on the stub. A panic rather than `t.Fatalf` on purpose: threading a `*testing.T` through would mean changing `newFakeEvalStore`'s signature and every call site, for no gain over a message that already says everything |
| 4.33 | `was_flapping` is wired but never asserted end to end | done | 2026-10-26, and it was worse than unasserted: `alertOut`, the API tests' own decode struct, **did not carry the field at all**, so no test could have read it even by accident. The column itself is covered by the store (`TestExecuteOpenSeedsWasFlappingFromCheckState`); what nothing covered is the single line that hands it to a client, `WasFlapping: a.WasFlapping` in `toAlertDTO` — flipped, dropped or hard-wired, it would have shipped silently, because `seedOpenAlert` writes no `check_states` row and `InsertAlert` seeds the column FROM that row. The test seeds two tuple states before opening, one flapping and one not, on two hosts: a lone true assertion passes just as well against a hard-wired true, so the pair is what makes the field prove it is reading something. Both the detail and the list path are checked, since a divergence between them would be its own bug. Verified negatively by wiring the assignment to false |
| 4.34 | `AlertsPage`'s own tests were written after the page, not before | won't do | Process note, not a code defect: TDD was not followed at the page level (component-level red was real; the page-level tests came after). Closed 2026-10-26 as a record rather than as work: a lapse in the order two files were written cannot be undone by writing anything now, and the thing the order exists to protect — coverage — is in place (`AlertsPage.test.tsx`, 18 cases, beside `AlertRow` 30, `AlertTimeline` 15, `HostAlertsCard` 9). Re-deleting the page to re-derive it from red would buy the appearance of process and nothing else. The row stays in the backlog as the record it is |
| 4.35 | Task 3's migration commit also carries the sqlc-generated `Alert` and `AlertEvent` structs from migration 00049 (Task 1) | won't do | **Nothing to do, 2026-10-26.** It describes a commit already in history: the generated types landed one commit later than the migration that produced them. Correct code, bundled awkwardly, on a branch that is not rewritten. Recording the observation is the whole value — it is a note about how a past commit was cut, not a defect in the tree |
| 4.36 | A voided acknowledgement reads as acknowledged again once the severity falls back | done | Settled by v0.4d's design decision 2, forced by escalation needing one unambiguous answer to "is this acknowledged" rather than two that disagree: `alerts.acknowledged_severity` is now cleared when the `ack_voided` event is written (migration 00057 relaxed `alerts`' CHECK constraint from a biconditional to a one-directional form to permit it, keeping `acknowledged_at` as the record that an acknowledgement happened at all). So an alert acked at `warn`, risen to `crit`, fallen back to `warn` now reads as unacknowledged everywhere — the list filter, the host card, the timeline — matching what its own timeline already showed, and its escalation chain keeps climbing instead of reading as settled. Accepted cost, named in the decision: an alert flapping across a severity boundary has to be acknowledged more than once |
| 4.37 | `AlertsPage` can show "No alerts match this view." and "Load more" at the same time | done | 2026-10-26, exactly as the row prescribed: the empty-state line is suppressed while `alerts.hasNextPage`, so "nothing matched" is only ever said once the cursor is exhausted and it is actually known. The cause stands and is correct — the `muted` filter is applied in Go AFTER the page is cut (`alerts.Service.List`), so an empty page with `next_cursor` set is a legitimate answer and following the cursor yields the full set — it was only the copy that contradicted itself |
| 4.38 | `writeAlertAction`'s transaction is only "its own" by caller discipline | done | Done 2026-11-24 on `fix/small-items-3.80-4.38-4.41` (`1834996f`): `DB.Begin` inside `WithReadTx` now returns a savepoint, as inside `WithWriteTx`, so a nested store's Commit or Rollback never ends the outer read transaction; a write there fails with Postgres' read-only error (25006), which the doc comments of `Begin` and `writeAlertAction` now say. (`WithOrg` inside `WithReadTx` still opens its own transaction; unreachable today and documented on `boundTx`). Originally: `db.DB.Begin` returns the SHARED transaction inside `WithReadTx` (`db/scope.go`), so the `Commit` in `writeAlertAction` would end a caller's outer read transaction early for every other store reading through it. Unreachable today — `WithReadTx` has one user, the inventory renderer, which never writes an alert — and the doc comment now says so rather than claiming otherwise; the row exists so the first caller that puts an alert write inside a read transaction is not the one to discover it |
| 4.39 | A reversible operator action splits an alert episode in two | done | Done 2026-11-25 (`feat/3.13g-4.39-plugin-key-decisions-reopen-ack`, owner decision: adopt within one hour): a tuple alert that opens within one hour after the subject's latest episode closed `stale` adopts its acknowledgement (unless the new severity is above the acknowledged one) and its snooze (timed, still running; until-change only at the same severity), records `continues_alert_id` (migration 00120) and says so on its timeline. Originally: Un-monitoring a host, disabling a check, or editing a monitoring-group selector so the host drops out of it changes the resolved set, so the tuple becomes unmeasured, its alert closes `stale`, and the next tick that judges it `warn`/`crit` again opens a NEW episode — losing the acknowledgement, the snooze and the peak to the closed row. Not a false close (nothing measures the tuple meanwhile) and strictly better than the previous behaviour of staying open for ever, but an operator who toggles a check off and on again has to acknowledge twice. Decide whether a re-open within some window should adopt the previous episode's acknowledgement, or whether splitting is simply the honest answer |
| 4.40 | The alert list's `limit` query parameter advertises no bounds in OpenAPI | done | 2026-10-26, both routes together as the row asked. `minimum:"1" maximum:"100" default:"20"` on `alertListInput.Limit` and `hostListInput.Limit` — the same three tags the notify, schedule and trigger list inputs already carried, so this closes an inconsistency as much as a gap. `default` states `clampLimit`'s own zero case rather than changing it (20 is each package's `DefaultListLimit`), so an absent parameter resolves to exactly what it always did. What DOES change: `?limit=5000` is now a 422 instead of a silently trimmed page of 100 — a client asking for 5000 and receiving 100 with no signal cannot tell a short page from the end of the list. The test asserts the document and the behaviour together, because a tag nobody enforces describes nothing. The generated SPA client moved by one line (the stale "the server clamps it" prose); openapi-typescript does not emit numeric constraints as types, so the document itself is where the bounds live and where the test reads them |

## v0.4d follow-ups

A deferred limitation recorded while building escalation chains
(2026-10-16). Found during implementation and left deliberately, not an
oversight left in a commit message. Source:
`.superpowers/sdd/2026-10-16-v0.4d-escalation-chains/progress.md`.

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 4.41 | The escalation clock samples a maintenance occurrence at two instants, not over an interval | done | Done 2026-11-24 on `fix/small-items-3.80-4.38-4.41` (`17fde380`, `ec7a8e8f`, `a8457ccd`): for a chain whose step is naively due, the follower asks `alerts.SQLStore.LatestCoverageEnd` for the latest end of any window stretch covering the host in (now − delay, now] and moves the anchor there (design decision 5); no previous-tick state or migration needed. A stretch counts only if it ended after its window was created. Owner-visible consequence, kept per decision 5 and in `docs/upgrades.md`: a window recurring more often than a step's delay holds that step while it recurs. Originally: `eligible_since`'s maintenance anchor (design decision 5's third bullet) is computed by asking whether an occurrence covers the chain at one sampled instant — the chain's own anchor — rather than across the gap since the last tick. A maintenance window that both opens and closes strictly between two consecutive ticks is therefore never seen as having covered the alert at all, so the next rung's delay is measured from an anchor that is too old and fires early. The failure is one-directional and bounded: a rung can fire sooner than the design intends, never later, and the chain is never silently abandoned — "too early," not "never." Flagged during Task 6's implementation and re-verified at Task 9, where it remains untested by design (the e2e gate does not exercise maintenance at all). Closing it needs an interval-aware occurrence query — did any occurrence cover any instant in `[last tick, this tick]` — in place of the current point sample at `now` |
| 4.42 | A re-enabled check's alert opens at its old state's start | done | Done 2026-11-25 (`fix/4.42-3.9b-fresh-since-smart-standby`, owner decision: start fresh): the evaluation marks a state row when its tuple goes unmeasured (`check_states.unmeasured_at`, migration 00121, stamped only when later than the row's last judgement); a marked row counts as no previous state, so since, candidates, clear holds and the flap count restart and a new alert opens at the first new reading. Originally: Found 2026-11-25 in the 4.39 review: the evaluator keeps an unmeasured tuple's check_states row, so when a disabled check (or an un-monitored host) is measured again its state keeps the old `Since` and the new alert's `opened_at` is that old moment — before the previous episode's `closed_at` (observed: opened 08:58:57, previous closed 09:02:57). Happens with or without 4.39's adoption. Decide whether a tuple that comes back from unmeasured starts a fresh `Since` (or the opening is clamped to the previous close) |
